<!-- Table 2 -->
| Brief History of CHM Formulas | Brief History of CHM Formulas |
| --- | --- |
| Stage 1:  Beginning   (方剂学)<br>Qin and Han Dynasties (4th Century B.C. to 3rd Century A.D.) [Bamboo Book]<br>Formulas for Fifty-two Ailments (Wu Shi Er Bing Fang) 403 BC - 221 BC<br>280 simple formulas w/o formula names, no mention of 5E, zangfu, meridians<br>189 are formulas of 2 or more ingredients, the rest are single herb formulas.<br>Yellow Emperor’s Inner Classic (Huang Di Nei Jing) [221 BC-1st/2nd Century AD] <br>13 formulas, still quite rudimentary.<br>Outline of the rudiments of a therapeutic strategy based on the tastes of herbs, e.g. acrid disperses, sour retains, sweet moderates, bitter strengthens, and salty softens. [Each taste enters different meridians]<br>Sets up modern TCM theories [Bible of TCM]<br>Also some discussion of how the tastes relate to Zang Fu, etc.<br>Discussion of Cold-induced Disorders & Miscellaneous Diseases <br>(Shang Han Za Bing Lun written by Zhang Zhong Jing [Most Important Doctor] 150-219 A.D. late Han Dynasty)<br>314 formulas with names, dosage & method of preparation of herbs & formulas [Classic Formulas]<br>Established current Format:  Patient, tongue & pulse, history, differentiation, diagnosis, treatment principles<br>True ancestor of all formularies & has had a tremendous influence on CHM throughout East Asia to the present day. Dr. Zhang was the first who completed the system named as Theory, strategy, formulas, and substances (Li Fa Fang Yao). <br>Edited about 50 years after by Wang Shu He, who divided book into 2 parts <br>Discussion of Cold-induced Disorders (Shang Han Lun): External diseases<br>Essentials from Gold Cabinet (Jin Gui Yao Lue):  Internal Disorders. <br>Detailed system of diagnosis that focuses on the six stages of disease. <br>Dr. Zhang was first to identify the condition of a patient (differentiation) with a particular formula used to treat that condition, i.e. the name of the formula itself is another way of stating the diagnosis. For example, instead of saying that a patient is suffering from a Shao Yang disorder, one could also describe it as a Xiao Chai Hu Tang (Minor Bupleurum Decoction) disorder. The text does contain rather specific instructions for preparing the formulas, among which are decoctions, drafts, pills, and medicinal wine.  Most of the formulas from these works by Dr. Zhang are still in use by many modern practitioners.<br>Liu Wei Di Huang Wan ?<br>Qian Yi ?<br><br>Stage 2:  Booming<br>Jin, Sui, Tang, and Song Dynasties (3rd to 13th Centuries)<br>Emergency Formulas to Keep Up One Sleeve [Zhou Hou Bei Ji Fang written by Ge Hong (281-341), Jin dynasty]<br>Features the use of simple and inexpensive, yet effective 101 formulas or so.<br>Thousand Ducat Formulas (Qian Jin Yao Fang) 30 volumes & <br>Supplement to the Thousand Ducat Formulas (Qian Jin Yi Fang) 30 volumes written by Sun Si Miao (581-682) around 625 AD<br>Over 6500 formulas collected from minorities, folks, foreign doctors and monks were included in Dr. Sun’s books, which have had a major impact on later generations. Many of these formulas are still in use, and many other commonly-used formulas are variations on those which were first devised by Dr. Sun.<br>Arcane Essentials from the Imperial Library (Wai Tai Bi Yao) <br>40 volumes compiled in 752 AD (after 20 years hard work in the Library) by Wang Tao (702-772 AD) based on previously-published Chinese works as well as some foreign texts before 8th century<br>More than 6000 formulas categorized into 1104 according to the type disorder they were designed to treat. Wang Tao, an official of the national library, made a great contribution in keeping record of a lot of formulas from books lost long ago.  <br>Imperial Grace Formulary of the Tai Ping Era (Tai Ping Sheng Hui Fang) 100 volumes compiled and published between 982 and 992 under imperial auspices – 16,834 formulas categorized into 1670 chapters with discussions on diagnosis and pathology of conditions.<br>Imperial Medical Encyclopedia (Sheng Ji Zong Lu): 200 volumes compiled by a staff of doctors under imperial orders and published about 1111 – 1117 AD. Near 20,000 formulas discussed with etiology, pathology and differentiations under thirteen departments, i.e. internal, pediatric, gynecological, surgical, dermatological, EENT…. | In addition, monographic formula books appeared in this period, such as <br>Liu Juan Zi Gui Yi Fang (Formulas Left Over by Ghosts written by Gong Qing Xuan, the earliest extant medical book on surgery) <br>Fu Ren Da Quan Liang Fang (Complete Effective Formulas for Women’s Diseases written by Chen Zi Ming)<br>Wai Ke Jing Yao (Essential of Surgery written by Chen Zi Ming) <br>Xiao Er Yao Zheng Zhi Jue (Key to Differentiations and Formulas for Children’s Diseases written by Qian Yi)<br>Jing Xiao Chan Bao (Tested Treasures in Obstetrics written by Zan Yin, the earliest book on obstetrics in TCM existing), and so forth.<br><br>Stage 3:  Deepening<br>Jin and Yuan Dynasties (12th to 14th Centuries)<br>Four great figures:  Representatives of Jin Yuan Four Schools of Thought<br>Liu Yuan Su (also named as Liu Shou Zhen, or Liu He Jian, about 1120-1200) <br>Cooling School, which propounded that disorders were caused by excessive heat in the body and advocated the use of cold herbs. <br>He had much influence on the School of Febrile Diseases [4 Greats] in the Ming and Qing Dynasties. Liu’s representative formulas: <br>Fang Feng Tong Sheng San, Shao Yao Tang, Shuang Jie San.<br>Zhang Cong Zheng (also named as Zhang Zi He, about 1156-1228) <br>Purging School [Gong Xia Pai], which propounded that disorders were caused by the pathogens and advocated the use of draining herbs, such as diaphoretics, emetics and purgatives and thus he started the <br>Started Attacking or Purging School. Zhang’s representative formulas: <br>San Sheng San, Mu Xiang Bing Lang Wan.<br>Very aggressive, not good for deficient types<br>Li Gao (also named as Li Dong Yuan, Li Ming Zhi, about 1180-1252)  <br>Earth Tonifying School, which propounded that diseases, apart from external disorders, were mainly caused by internal injury in spleen and stomach and advocated that treating these internal disorders with tonifying spleen & stomach and boosting Qi. <br>Li was considered as the founder of the Earth-tonifying School. <br>His masterpiece was Pi Wei Lun (Treatise on Spleen & Stomach), and representative formulas: <br>Bu Zhong Yi Qi Tang, Sheng Yang Yi Wei Tang. <br>Zhu Dan Xi (also named as Zhu Zhen Heng, about 1280-1358) <br>Yin Enriching School: Indulgence was the root of all disorders and exhorted the value of Yin tonics in the prevention and treatment of diseases. He advocated that Yang was often in excess while Yin was often in deficiency. He was considered as the founder of Yin-enriching School.<br>Masterpiece was Ju Fang Fa Hui (An Expounding of the Imperial Grace Formulary of the Tai Ping Era) and Dan Xi Xin Fa (Zhu Dan Xi’s Medical Secret), and representative formulas: Da Bu Yin Wan, Hu Qian Wan.<br>Minister fire always making trouble<br>In addition, there appeared some books that included the Analysis of Formulas, such as:<br>Zhu Jie Shang Han Lun (Commentary on Shang Han Lun), <br>Shang Han Ming Li Lun (Deliberation of Shang han Lun) by Cheng Wu Ji (about 1062-1155).<br><br>Stage 4:  Refining and Developing<br>Ming and Qing Dynasties (14th to 19th Centuries)<br>Zheng Zhi Zhen Sheng (Standardized Patterns and Treatments):  Compiled by Wang Ken Tang (also named as Wang Yu Tai or Wang Sun An, 1549-1613), in which Wang developed a synthesis of the approaches of the various schools by utilizing concepts from both Nei Jing & Shang Han Lun.  This book included the rich experiences of the previous doctors and classified prescriptions according to six departments (febrile, internal, dermatological, pediatric, obstetrical), and largest circulation of medical books in 17th century. <br>Jing Yue Quan Shu (Jing Yue’s Complete Works) in 64 volumes:  Written by Zhang Jing Yue in 1624 (also named as Zhang Jie Bin, about 1563-1640), in which all aspects of medicine covered (febrile, internal, gynecological, pediatric, dermatological, herbal, ancient and 2 volume of 186 new formulas), the essence of various schools extracted and analyzed, patterns differentiation along with treatment principles and formulas emphasized.<br>Classified the formulas into 8 groups under 8 strategies: Bu, He, Gong, San, Han, Re, Gu, Yin. <br>Zhang advocated theory:  “Yang is never in excess, Yin is never sufficient”. Thus he tended to use warm and tonifying herbs:  Shu Di Huang was among his favorite herbs; His representative formulas are <br>Da Bu Yuan Jian, Yu Nu Jian, Ji Chuan Jian. |


<!-- Table 3 -->
| Brief History of CHM Formulas [Continued] | Brief History of CHM Formulas [Continued] |
| --- | --- |
| Pu Ji Fang (Formulas for Universal Applications): Written by Teng Hong & Liu Chun under the patronage of one of the emperors of Ming Dynasty and issued at the beginning of 15th century. This book was considered as biggest collection of formulas (61,739 formulas with 239 illustrations).<br>Analytic Collection of Medical Formulas (Yi Fang Ji Jie) – Written by Wang Ang and published in 1682. The book listed about 700 formulas, classified into 21 chapters such as tonifying, releasing, vomiting, draining, inducing, harmonizing, Qi regulating, blood regulating, wind dispersing, cold dispelling, eye brightening, bug killing…. [Very comprehensive & classified well]<br>Under each listed formula there were the name, ingredients, indication, explanations and experiences from the practitioners of previous dynasties.<br>Emphasized relationship between formulas (herbs), zang fu & meridians.<br>Treatise on Warm Pathogens (Wen Re Lun) – Lectured by Ye Tian Shi and edited by his disciple in 1746, a book on the diagnosis and treatment for febrile diseases in which the <br>Developed 4-level diagnostic system [Wei, Qi, Ying, Xue]. <br>Representative formulas: Yin Qiao San, Sang Ju Ying, Qing Ying Tang. <br>Systematic Differentiation of Warm Diseases (Wen Bing Tiao Bian) – Written by Wu Ju Tong (also named as Wu Tang) in 1798, in which Wu expanded on Ye’s work by developing the 3-Burner diagnostic system. For example, based on Zhang Zhong Jing’s formula zhi gan cao tang, Wu developed jia jian fu mai tang, yi jia fu mai tang, er jia fu mai tang, san jia fu mai tang, and da ding feng zhu series.    <br>Fu Qing Zhu’s Obstetrics and Gynecology (Fu Qing Zhu Nu Ke) – Written by Fu Shan (1607-1684) and first published in 1827. The book consists of 77 chapters plus Postpartum Care including Ob-Gyn’s Dx and Tx with easy-understanding language and practical formulas. <br>Formula Jingles (Tang Tou Ge Jue) – Written by Wang Ang and published in 1694. Over 300 formulas were included with their names, ingredients, indications, and some modifications in the form of jingle, so that it might be easier for beginners to learn by heart. | Medical Comprehension (Yi Xue Xin Wu) – Written by Cheng Guo Peng (also named Cheng Zhong Ling) and published in 1732, in which <br>Eight Strategies [8 Principles] was first elaborated: han [sweating], tu [vomiting], xia [downward draining], he [harmonize], wen, qing, xiao, bu, and internal, skin, ob-gyn, eent disorders were covered. This book is very concise and practical and consequently referred to by later practitioners.<br>Zhong Yi Feng Ji Da Ci Dian:  96, 592 Formulas<br><br>Jing Fang [经方] Classical Formula School<br>Formulas in books by Zhang Zhong Jing, Shang Han Lun and Jin Gui yao Lue, which has been used for about two thousands years and contain fewer ingredients in one formula.<br>Elegantly formed<br>Clear methodology<br>Have to worship & follow the past, the Classic Rules<br><br>Shi Fang [时方] Latter-day Formula School<br>Formulas devised from the Song dynasty onward, and particularly those developed by practitioners of the Warm-febrile Disease School, and usually contain more ingredients in one formula.<br>Diseases have changed since Shang Han Lun was written<br>Clinical standards have changed<br>Effectiveness first<br><br>Review and Think<br>How many stages in the history of development of CHM formulas?<br>Who are the major figures, and what are their representative works in CHM formulary history?<br>What are the main systems for categorization of the formulas<br>What are the common-used forms for administration, and their dis/advantages?<br>What are the differences between jing fang and shi fang? |

<!-- Table 5 -->
| Introduction to Traditional Chinese Herbal Medicine | Introduction to Traditional Chinese Herbal Medicine |
| --- | --- |
| Developed by Dr. Zhang Zhong Jing in Shang Han Lun (Qing Dynasty) <br>[3rd century AD]<br>Diagnosis  Strategy  Formula  Herb [8 Methods]<br>Diagnosis:  Comes from analysis & synthesis of symptoms & signs<br>Strategy:  Based on Diagnosis<br>Formula:  Under guidance of strategy<br>Herbs vary from symptoms & signs<br>Identify the patient’s problem, treatment strategy, formula that incorporates the strategy  specific herbs that constitute the formula will naturally follow<br><br>Eight Traditional Strategies (Ba Fa)<br>The 8 methods exist in any single method. <br>Likewise, a myriad of methods exist within the 8 methods.<br>All 8 Methods are used today, except vomiting.<br><br>Sweating (Han Fa):  Induces sweating by stimulating & disseminating the LU Qi, regulating & facilitating the interaction between Ying & Wei Qi, so that the pores open & pathogenic influences in the exterior or other superficial level of the body can be released with sweat.  Applied in 2 ways:<br>Acrid & Warming:  Ma Huang Tang<br>Acrid & Cooling:  Ying Qiao San [Honeysuckle<br>Vomiting (Tu Fa):  [Pathogen above middle jiao]  Stimulates the Stomach so that phlegm, stagnant food or toxic stuff stuck in the throat, chest, or stomach cavity can be expelled through the mouth.<br>Melon Pedicle Powder [Gua Di San]<br>Downward Draining (Xia Fa):  [Pathogen below middle jiao]  Induces defecation to cleanse bowels & expel substantive pathogenic influences through rectum.  This method is very strong, only used for ejecting substantive pathogenic stuff from the body.  Applied in 4 ways:<br>Purge Heat Accumulation:  Major Order the Qi Decoction<br>Warm Yang & Guide Out Accumulation:  Rhubarb & Prepared Aconite Decoction<br>Moisten Intestines & Unblock Bowels:  Hemp Seed Pill<br>Drive Out Excess Water:  Ten JuJuBe Decoction<br>Harmonizing (He Fa) [Two best friends start fighing]  Harmonizes or regulates functions of different levels & Organs.  Applied in 5 ways:  <br>Harmonize Shao Yang:  Minor Bupleurum Decoction<br>Vent th Membrane Source:  Reach the Membrane Source Decoction<br>Harmonize GB & ST:  Artemisia Annua & Scutellariae Decoction<br>Harmonize LV & SP:  Rambling Powder<br>Harmonize ST & Intestines:  Pinellia Decoction to Drain Epigastrium<br>Mu Yuan:  Special concept in TCM  Special place where pathogens like to hide | Warming (Wen Fa):  Warms interior & unblocks meridians to dispel cold from interior or meridians  Restore functions of Yang Qi.  Applied in 3 ways:<br>Warm meridians & disperse cold:  Dang Gui Decoction for Frigid Extremities<br>Warm Middle & dispel cold:  Li Zhong Decoction<br>Restore & Revive Yang:  Ginseng & Prepared Aconite Decoction<br>Clearing (Qing Fa):  Clear Heat & Drain Fire to eliminate Heat, Fire & their associated toxicity from body.  Usually applied in 4 ways:<br>Qi Level:  White Tiger Decoction<br>Clear Heat from Ying Level & Cool Blood:  Clear Ying Level Decoction<br>Clear Heat & Relieve Toxicity:  Coptidis Decoction to Relieve Toxicity<br>Clear Heat from Organs:  <br>Honeysuckle, Forsythia & Puffball San<br>Drain the White Powder<br>Clear the Stomach Powder<br>Drain the Yellow Powder<br>Jade Women Decoction<br>Guide out the Red Powder<br>Gentiana Long Dan Cao Decoction to Drain Liver<br>Drain the Green Pill<br>Reducing (Xiao Fa):  Gradually reduces or eliminates clumping or accumulation due to Stagnation of Qi, Blood, fluid, parasites.  Applied in 6 ways:<br>Eliminate Food Stagnation:  Preserve Harmony Pill<br>Reduce Accumulation:  Cinnamon Twig & Poria Pill<br>Transform Phlegm:  <br>Er Chen Tang [Two Cured Decoction]<br>Warm GB Decoction<br>Eliminate childhood nutritional impairment:  Strenghten Spleen Pill<br>Kill Parasite:  Mume Pill [Mumps:  Can cause infertility in men]<br>Reduce Sores & Disperse Abscesses:  Universal Benefit Decoction to Eliminate Toxin<br>Tonifying (Bu Fa):  Tonifies by enriching, nourishing, augmenting or replenishing weak or deficient aspects of the body.  Applied in 4 ways:<br>Tonify Qi:  Four Gentlemen Decoction<br>Tonify Blood:  Four substance Decoction<br>Tonify Yin:  Six Rehmannia Pill<br>Tonify Yang:  KD Qi Pill |

| Composition of the Formulas:  Principles for making up formulas [4 Ranks] | Composition of the Formulas:  Principles for making up formulas [4 Ranks] | Formula Modifications: Replace bad tasting ingredients with acceptable taste <br>Huang lian<br>Ku shen<br>Wu ling zhi<br><br>Special Treatments for Decocting<br>Decocted First (Xian Jian):  <br>Fu Zi, Chuan Wu, Shi Jue Ming, Cao Wu, Long Gu, Mu Li, Ci Shi, Zhen Zhu, <br>Gui Ban, Bie Jia, Shi Gao, Shui Niu Jiao, Dai Zhe Shi, Si Gua Luo<br>Added Near End (Huo Xia) [Aromatics]:  <br>Bo He, Mu Xiang, Sha Ren, Bai Dou Kou, Qing Hao, Da Huang<br>Decocted In Gauze (Bao Jian) [Nasty Stuff, small seeds, flowers]:  <br>Xuan Fu Hua, Che Qian Zi, Liu Yi San, Bi Yu San, Chi Shi Zhi<br>Separately Decocted Or Simmered [Expensive Stuff] (Ling Jian, Ling Dun)<br>Ren Shen, Xi Yang Shen, Lu Rong<br>Dissolved in the strained decoction (Rong Hua):<br>E Jiao, Yi Tang<br>Taken With The Strained Decoction (Chong Fu): <br>Chuan Bei Mu, San Qi, Niu Huang, Zhu Li<br><br>Timing and Manner of Administration [Think about Patients]<br>Taken about one hour before meals generally<br>Take herbs for specific organs at optimal time according to clock<br>Ingredients that irritate Stomach:  take after meals<br>Rich, cloying tonics:  take on an empty Stomach.<br>Calm spirit:  taken before going to bed<br>Malarial conditions: take 1-3 hours before an attack on a regular basis if timing is regular<br>Releasing Exterior:  take while warm<br>Taken warm causes nausea or vomiting: take cool<br>Patent herbs:  take at a specified time as needed<br>Ji Ming San:  take as soon as one awakens<br>Toxic formulas: start with a small dose, slowly increase<br><br>Measurement:<br>1 liang两 = 30 g克<br>1 qian钱 = 3 g<br>1 fen分 = 0.3 g<br><br>Dosage:<br>Common dosage for most of the available substances:  3-5 qian, [9-15 g]. |

| Among the 8 strategies, this belongs to the tonifying (补法).<br>Formulas in this chapter are used to treat qi xu, blood xu, qi and blood xu, yin xu, and yang xu.  Between qi and blood, yin and yang, tonifying one will have a salutary effect on the other.<br>In “Discussion of the Spleen and Stomach” Li Dong Yuan said: “For blood xu, use ren shen to tonify, because when the yang qi is exuberant, it will generate yin blood”.<br>In “Collected Treatises of Jing-Yue” Zhang Jing Yue said: “To tonify the yang well, you must search for yang within the yin… To tonify the yin well, you must search for yin within the yang.”<br>Many formulas that tonify the yang (qi) therefore contain ingredients that tonify the yin (blood), and vice versa.<br>For deficiency, tonify the mother is often applied as a strategy for tonification. E.g. tonify water to tonify wood. | Side-effects often develop when tonics are prescribed for individuals who are not suffering from xu – [fever, HA, rash, pimples, insomnia, irritability, and digestive upset]<br>Too deficient to be tonified [虚不受补] the rich, cloying nature of tonifying herbs places a burden on the digestive system. It is usually necessary to add ingredients that promote digestion and assimilation. E.g. chen pi, sha ren.<br>Start with mild tonics, then gradually get stronger<br>Mild, chronic conditions require a smaller dosage than do acute, life-threatening conditions.<br>Tonifying formulas: generally cooked for long period of time over a low flame (between 30’-1hr.), and taken on an empty stomach to realize their full effect.<br>Relationship of Qi & Blood  Tonify Qi, Tonify Blood |

| Bleeding (hemorrhage, spillage of blood) manifestations varies – vomiting of blood (hematemesis), nosebleeds (epistaxis), coughing of blood (hemoptysis), blood in the stool (hemafecia), blood in the urine (hematuria), excessive menstruation, and uterine bleeding. <br> | TCM bleeding types may be further differentiated in accordance with the various Zang & Fu organs involved; the etiological factors of cold, heat, xu, and shi; the location of the bleeding; and whether the disorder is acute or chronic. Among them, the most common causes of bleeding as the primary symptom are the reckless movement of hot blood, wherein the blood is forced out of the vessels, and Xu, wherein the body is unable to retain the blood within vessels. |

| Acute bleeding: priority is to first stop bleeding & resolve disharmony later<br>Chronic bleeding: treat symptom (bleeding) & the underlying condition simultaneously <br>Formulas include astringent, hemostatic ingredients as chief, and heat-clearing or qi-tonifying or other ingredients to address associated sx/underlying factors.<br>Use stop-bleeding formulas short-term<br>For bleeding from the upper jiao: <br>shi heat:  add niu xi, zhi shi to guide blood down; <br>xu cold in middle jiao: add ban xia, pao jiang to warm & descend Blood [Excess pushes blood up  guide back to where it’s supposed to be]<br>For bleeding from the lower jiao: add jing jie, sheng ma, huang qi, ren shen to raise the Yang and tonify Qi  [chronic uterine bleeding, hemorrhoids, colitis], tonify Qi so that blood stays in vessels, raise Qi so that it doesn’t collapse down | For bleeding from the upper jiao, avoid ma huang, gui zhi, sheng ma, chai hu [ascending herbs  disperse]<br>For bleeding from the lower jiao, avoid niu xi, zhi shi, da huang, mang xiao [descending herbs, purging]<br>Dang Gui, Chuan Xiong, Dan Shen are often chosen to arrest bleeding d/t blood stasis, to prevent blood stasis from forming as the stop-bleeding ingredients are often astringent & may stagnate Blood flow<br>For prolonged bleeding, Dang Gui Bu Xue Tang [Dang Gui (1) & Huang Qi (5)] should be given to tonify qi & blood or added into the basic formula if not already included<br> |

| Wind syndromes are conditions involving signs & symptoms that present the characteristics of wind – <br>1) wind is a Yang pathogenic factor that tends to disperse, moving upward & outward. It can therefore easily invade Yang, Exterior. <br>2) the nature of wind is to arise quickly, migrate & change rapidly. <br>3) wind tends to make things move. <br>4) wind is the initial exogenous pathogenic factor in causing disorders <br> | Treatment Principles & considerations<br>External Wind: use acrid, dispersing & diaphoretics as chief ingredients to disperse pathogens from Surface<br>When treating E-W, do not use sweet nourishing or sedating herbs since they may cause EPI to transfer to the interior<br>Internal Wind: use nourishing or sedating ingredients as the chief according to specific etiology<br>When treating I-W, do not use acrid dispersing herbs since they may cause Lr Yang to rise & impair Yin, Blood & Fluids, thus aggravating symptoms<br>When treating I-W, herbs that invigorate & tonify Blood are often added since “ to treat Wind, first treat Blood. Once Blood is moving, Wind will stop by itself.”<br>Because wind invasion is often associated with other pathogens such as heat, damp, cold, or dry, it is important to address these |

| Damp syndromes involve either an invasion of exogenous pathogenic damp or an internal dysfunction of fluids metabolism, i.e. distribution & transformation, arising from impaired SP (transporting & transforming), LU (dispersing, descending & purifying) or KD  (warming & transforming) functions.<br> | Damp is heavy, turbid & stagnant by nature. It tends to sink & stubbornly linger. Damp is thus classified as a Yin pathogen that tends to obstruct qi function & impair the flow of the body’s Yang qi. <br>Sp is Zang most affected by damp.<br>Tongue coating reflects GI system<br>GI Endoscopes indicate amount of inflammation in GI system |


<!-- Table 540 -->
| Damp location | symptoms | Damp type | symptoms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Skin | Edema, a feeling of heaviness & tightness | Cold-damp | Heaviness in the body & head, joint stiffness & pain, edema (especially in the lower part of the body), loose stools |
| Meridians & collaterals | Joint pain, stiffness or numbness, swollen joints, heaviness of the extremities | Damp-heat | Jaundice, painful & burning urination, low-grade fever, thirst with no desire to drink, itchy skin, fetid leucorrhea |
| Middle Jiao | Abdominal distension, nausea, vomiting, loose stools, poor appetite, sweet or greasy taste in the mouth | Wind-damp | Chills & fever, heaviness in the body & head, stiffness & pain in the joints, superficial edema |
| Lower Jiao | Difficult urination, leucorrhea | Water-damp | Pitting edema, difficult urination |
|  |  | Summer-heat with damp | Fever, sweating, vomiting, fullness in the abdomen & epigastrium, diarrhea, anorexia, feeling of heaviness in the body |


<!-- Table 541 -->
| Treatment Principles & Considerations | Treatment Principles & Considerations |
| --- | --- |
| Since damp conditions vary widely, treatment should be based on the nature & location of the condition, the particular Zang-fu involved, and whether there are other pathogenic factors complicating the condition. The specific Tx methods are:<br>Damp in Upper Jiao/Exterior: diaphoretic herbs to release damp from surface<br>Damp in Middle Jiao: bitter &/or aromatic herbs to resolve damp, awaken SP<br>Damp in Lower Jiao:  herbs to promote urination & drain water-damp | Damp with cold – herbs to warm Yang, dispel cold & resolve water-damp<br>Damp with heat – herbs to clear heat & dry damp<br>Water-damp accumulation – harsh herbs to strongly purge water-damp (only in patients with a strong constitution) [ascites], short period of time<br>Damp with wind – herbs to dispel wind & expel damp [Bi syndrome]<br>Nei Jing:  Open Ghost Door & Cleansing Palace |


<!-- Table 542 -->
| Cautions & contraindications | Cautions & contraindications |
| --- | --- |
| Herbs that expel damp are pungent, warming & drying on the whole. Damp-expelling formulas are consequently contraindicated in medium to severe Yin & Fluids xu, unless modified by adding fluid-generating, dryness-relieving herbs such as hua shi & lu gen<br> | In cases where edema accompanies with chronic dz or pregnancy, tonifying herbs should be added to the formula to support upright qi, e.g. bai zhu, huang qi<br>Add qi-regulating herbs to the formula because damp is a heavy, cloying pathogen that can easily generate qi stagnation |
